Grand Theft Auto VI Review - Outrageous Fortune

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Many superlatives will be heard in the discussion around Grand Theft Auto VI – biggest world, most activities, best graphics, deepest narrative – but its real triumph is the way all those features and others work in service of a cohesive, believable immersion in a criminal fantasy that is hard to grasp until you’ve played it for yourself.

Rockstar’s latest returns players to a dramatically expanded version of the Vice City seen in a prior game, now largely unrecognizable after real and in-game years have passed. Leonida is a joy to explore, filled with a wealth of activities, both mundane and illicit. The game’s design makes many ordinary tasks as compelling as the illegal ones. Go fishing off the boat, brew coffee to wake up, work out at the gym, scuba dive for long-lost treasure, dust crops, motocross in the mud, go dancing at the club. As you move between those activities, the world feels alive with minor events, from traffic accidents to shady individuals a little too interested in your walk to the ATM. GTAVI delights in life's minutiae, with animations, voice lines, and background characters that keep things fresh even dozens of hours into the story. The level of detail everywhere is astounding and, at times, overwhelming. Getting sidetracked is easy.

The focus on detail carries into the visuals. Lucia’s hair blows in the wind, and she absently pushes it back behind her ear. A drug den is littered with paraphernalia and empty containers. A shifty contact quirks a smile – your only hint that things are about to go south. Sweat glistens on your character’s skin after a hard workout, their breathing and heart rate noticeably heightened. Clouds roll together in the sky, creating realistic weather simulations. The commitment to an authentic simulated world is encompassing, thoughtful, and intentional, a testament to hand-crafted and meticulously painstaking development work, paying off in an absorbing play-space that is hard to step away from.

While the day-to-day simulation is fascinating, it also adds pacing and counterpoint to the explosive march of major missions, narrative threads, and set-piece encounters that drive the game forward. Not even one of these meticulously choreographed sequences deserves to be spoiled; they run the gamut from amusing shenanigans at a strip club to intense chases and escapes fueled by raging gunfights and explosions. Many of the best moments leverage an expert sense of musical accompaniment, weaving a slick original score alongside one of the most impressive and wide-ranging licensed soundtracks ever heard in a video game – often to surprisingly emotional effect.

Ranged combat is punchy and enjoyable; smart use of cover and time-slowing focus makes most battles manageable. On-foot navigation is in a similar boat – simple to pick up and uninterested in needless complexity or challenge; the focus is on choosing smart moments for stealth, knowing when to run and when to fight, and selecting the precise instant for a burst of sudden action. Driving is fast and intense, made more appealing by a huge variety of functionally distinct vehicles to enjoy. If there’s a fault to the core and optional story missions, it’s that many feel a bit on rails regarding where to go and what to do at each step. At the same time, that directed approach keeps the pace relentless, and the action and outcomes thrilling. Over many hours, the game morphs and throws new wrenches into the mix at key junctures, teasing and then playing out criminal experiences from petty theft to group heists to illicit spec ops, and punctuates those distinct vibes with memorable and singular moments.

Like its predecessors, GTAVI simultaneously lampoons and celebrates life in modern America, taking aim at targets like social media, wellness lifestyles, rap and music culture, manosphere content, and dozens more. The caricatures land so well because, in many cases, they’re (sadly) barely over the line of the absurdity we see reflected in real life, and because Rockstar so ably captures individual subcultures, dialects, and situations. Behind it all is a scathing critique of wealth inequality and the inherent unfairness of a system that sets most up to fail. It’s a bitter message delivered in honey-wrapped, exhilarating gameplay, but it shines through.

Of all its significant feats, the relationship between Lucia and Jason elevates the game to new heights, lending this installment a heart, tension, and emotional core missing from prior entries. This is, without a doubt, video gaming’s most richly drawn and compelling romance, featuring two leads who are immediately relatable and compelling to watch. Player actions dictate the strength of their bond, deepening the sense of engagement. Like the best crime fiction, you’re left surprised by how much you cheer for two people who regularly commit terrible acts. The two collectively suffer the slings and arrows of a world that always seems stacked against them; the fortune they amass is not a power grab, but a desperate attempt to cling to each other and come out the other side together, and the player can’t help but cheer them on and dread how fate might stand in the way.

Grand Theft Auto VI is a generational game that stands toe-to-toe with many of the great crime stories and satires across other media. It showcases both the entertainment value of interactive play and the current high-water mark for technical and artistic detail in games. Hunt for and count up all the minor faults or foibles you like, but then note how you just spent another hour lost in this remarkable escape into criminality, wanton violence, egregious sex, and the elusive potential of true love.

Alex Van Aken contributed to this article.

Kirk Cousins, Vikings reunion could be just getting started

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Quarterback Kirk Cousins will spend the rest of the 2026 season with the Vikings. That may not be the end of Kirko 2.0.

The Vikings have already expressed interest in keeping Cousins beyond the current season, we’re told. As we understand it, the sentiment was articulated casually and in passing before his rousing debut at Lambeau Field. And it was more of an indication that there will be discussions.

Of course, there will be a greater motivation to do something, now that it’s clear that Cousins is exactly what the doctor ordered for an offense that was ready for a visit from Dr. Kevorkian.

Even though Cousins is under contract through 2028, the deal was structured to spark a release after one season, with Cousins getting a $10 million payment on the back end aimed at sticking the Falcons with $8.7 million in 2026.

A base salary in 2028 of $78.655 million becomes fully guaranteed on the fifth day of the 2027 league year, along with a $1.345 million base salary in 2027. Although that works out to $80 million over two seasons (which puts the average closer to the bottom of the veteran quarterback pay scale than the top), the current structure imposes a ridiculously high cash and cap obligation for 2028, when he will be 40.

So the deal will need to be revised. And Cousins will have leverage, if he continues to do what he did on Sunday.

That said, he possibly will realize that it’s not the time to maximize his leverage. He did that in 2024, when he opted for a multi-year financial commitment that he mistakenly viewed to be a multi-year playing commitment. And then the Falcons pulled the rug out from under him by drafting Michael Penix Jr. roughly six weeks after the ink dried on the new deal.

And, frankly, the Vikings could decide to devise a plan for swallowing the proverbial poison pill and paying $40 million per year for two more season. The impact of Cousins’s return on the Minnesota offense would justify trying to devise a solution.

The best result would be for the two sides to work something out, at least for 2027. After 2023, the Vikings wanted to take a year-to-year approach with Cousins. Cousins wanted something more than that — even though the Rams and Matthew Stafford have been using the year-to-year thing for a while. In hindsight, a compromise would have been much better for both sides.

That lesson from the past should inform the future. As one source put it, sometimes it takes a breakup for everyone to appreciate each other.

It’s clear that, one week after Cousins returned to Minnesota, both sides have significant appreciation for each other. Which gives both sides a very strong motivation to find a way to keep it going at least for 2027.

Unless, of course, the Vikings win the Super Bowl this year and Cousins decides to walk off into the sunset with the trophy that will fit in that spot in his house in Michigan he has saved for it.

That would throw the Minnesota quarterback position back into disarray for 2027. But if it comes with the first Super Bowl win in franchise history, the Vikings would take that.

HBO’s Harry Potter Officially Confirms 1 Original Movie Star Is Returning

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

As Hogwarts slowly appears on the horizon, HBO's new Harry Potter adaptation now feels closer than ever. On December 25, and despite the ongoing controversy surrounding the Wizarding World's creator, J.K. Rowling​​​, the world will settle in for the ultimate Christmas present, as a brand-new, shiny adaptation of Harry Potter and the Philosopher's Stone debuts. Recently, HBO released the first full clip of the series, as the new students of Hogwarts celebrate Dumbledore (John Lithgow) and begin their first feast in the Great Hall.

Alas, the clip has proven far from satisfying for some fans, who have filed plenty of complaints about the show via social media. One of the most consistent complaints that has plagued HBO since marketing for the new show began is the overall tone and color palette. This clip has done little to soften those issues, with a supposed lack of whimsy and warmth, and the strange choice to replace the floating candles with standard lamps, causing outrage online. Much has been said, both good and bad, about the casting for the new Harry Potter adaptation. Just one name has survived from the original movie cast through to this new adaptation, as Warwick Davis, the man behind Charms Professor Flitwick and Goblin Griphook, returns.

In a previous interview, Davis gave insight into what it was like to return, first admitting that the "character has been one of my favorite characters to play in my career." "He’s a very warm, kind of likeable character, as far as I’m concerned. I actually based him a lot on some of my teachers," Davis revealed. "He’s the kind of teacher you could say you’d forgotten your homework and they wouldn’t mind. They’d say, ‘OK, don’t do it again, but don’t worry about it for now’." And what was it like to be asked to return? Davis replied:

"To be asked to come back and play him again for the series was a huge honour, and I’m excited to retell these stories because they’re fantastic stories."

The Wachowskis Are Trying to Get The Matrix Back From Skydance

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Twenty-seven years have passed since The Matrix was released in cinemas, changing the entertainment landscape in perhaps more ways than its creators, Lana and Lilly Wachowski, could have envisioned. The Matrix was part of a three-movie deal that the Wachowskis landed with Warner Bros., and the rights to the franchise​​​​​​​ remained firmly in the hands of the studio, recently absorbed by Skydance in a much-discussed merger. But things may change in the future.

As revealed by Eriq Gardner in an article for Puck, the Wachowskis have formally notified Warner Bros. that they are exercising their copyright termination rights. This means that, as early as February 2029​​​​​​​, Warner Bros. could lose the rights to produce and distribute new Matrix movies, which would go back to the creators. With a fifth movie currently in development, helmed by Drew Goddard, a legal battle could have a profound impact on the franchise.

The Wachowskis are not involved with The Matrix 5, but, according to Gardner's undisclosed sources, they "have been encouraging of the newest sequel and have even increased their involvement lately." This means that, despite Lilly's statement in September 2026 that she was "kinda done" with The Matrix, the sisters are still involved in some way​​​​​​​, and their relationship with Warner Bros. isn't necessarily bad.

In fact, the motion to terminate the copyright doesn't mean that Warner Bros. and the Wachowskis will engage in a legal battle over The Matrix. The studio could simply negotiate a fresh grant of rights with the Wachowskis and keep making The Matrix movies. If the issue goes to court, the key will be proving which party can truly be considered the "creator" of the franchise. The commonly reported origin story of the franchise, also mentioned by Gardner in his article, is that a Warner Bros. executive, Lorenzo di Bonaventura, commissioned the Wachowskis to write and direct the movie, after being impressed by their earlier work on Assassins and Bound. Commissioned work​​​​​​​ is ineligible for copyright termination, but proving that in court will be another matter​​​​​​​.

Overall, the most interesting takeaway from this story is that, in 2029, the Wachowskis could start working on a reboot of the Matrix franchise, freed from the shackles of corporate oversight. Meanwhile, there have been no updates about The Matrix 5 in a long time, but speculation points to a 2029 release​​​​​​​. As Morpheus said, "I do not see coincidence, I see providence."

Alan Cumming, Verity Power and Maria Friedman on Their Scottish, Spunky ‘My Fair Lady’: ‘It’s Not Your Granny’s’

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

When Alan Cumming was leaving drama school, everybody told him there was no point auditioning for Pitlochry Festival Theatre, because it only hired English actors. Four decades later he runs the place, and is closing his first season as artistic director by playing Professor Henry Higgins in “My Fair Lady” opposite Glaswegian actor Verity Power’s Eliza Doolittle.

“Having two Scottish people playing the leads in a show that is about the misapprehensions and the prejudices that people have about accent — I think we very much understand that, being Scottish,” Cumming tells Variety. Related Stories Amazon Debuts $30 Streaming Bundle That Includes Starz, BritBox, AMC+, MGM+ and PBS Masterpiece In One Place MS NOW Has 2026 Midterm Elections Coverage: Here's How to Watch Election Night Results Live Online Free

Lerner and Loewe’s “My Fair Lady” will run at the Perthshire venue from Nov. 21 to Dec. 31. Maria Friedman, the director behind the Tony-winning Broadway revival of Stephen Sondheim’s “Merrily We Roll Along,” is staging it, and the production is a co-presentation with Sonia Friedman Productions.

The show came about almost by accident. Cumming says he was approaching artists he admired, Friedman among them, and it was she who pitched the 1956 musical. For a theater of Pitlochry’s scale, a familiar title made sense as one of the season’s tentpoles, he says, with riskier work sitting alongside it.

“It’s not your granny’s ‘My Fair Lady,'” Cumming says. “It’s going to have an edge to it, and I feel like casting me, casting Verity, it’s a bit more spunky than you might expect.”

Friedman’s reading starts with the two leads as mirror images. She sees a piece about patriarchy, class, status and the lack of opportunity for women, but above all about authenticity, with Higgins and Eliza as “two sides of a coin.” Both react to the world viscerally, and neither has been polished out of themselves.

“I think that Higgins is definitely spectrum-y, without any question, and Eliza is emotionally incontinent, but they’re both passionate people about the things that they love,” Friedman says. “The further Eliza gets away from her original — she’s so full of chutzpah and life force — the more ladylike she becomes, the lonelier she becomes.”

Asked how she handles a relationship long sold as a romance but built on power and control, Friedman says, “It’s absolutely easy. We just don’t go there. Love comes in all shapes and sizes, and so I’m looking absolutely at a friendship. Two people who can rub along with each other, are better off with each other than without each other, still living their own lives.”

George Bernard Shaw famously resisted a romantic ending for “Pygmalion,” the 1913 play the musical is based on. Friedman acknowledges its influence but says she has to make the musical’s script work on its own terms, steering consciously away from “any point of ever thinking that there’s a love affair. It’s not going to happen.”

Nor is anyone sanding down Higgins. Cumming calls it a period piece about the patriarchy, in which a strong woman comes in and tries to bash it, and says he is serving both the musical and Shaw’s play. “I’m not interested in flattening things out to make them uncontentious,” he says. “I am interested in finding a way to play people so that you understand them.”

Friedman, who last saw “My Fair Lady” when she was 21, says she has no idea what other productions have done with the role. She sees a Higgins whose short fuse comes from repression, a man who at first treats Eliza as a scientific experiment until “that kind of microscope looking at this specimen flashes out in front of him into a human being, which is most alarming for him.” Cumming, she adds, “can turn on a sixpence: one minute he makes you want to cry, then you’re roaring with laughter.”

For Power, the shadow of Audrey Hepburn and Julie Andrews was something to study and then mostly set aside. A Hepburn devotee who has watched the 1964 film since she was about 7, she says she kept perhaps 20% of the screen Eliza. “There’s a big, fat 80% which just has to come from me, otherwise it’s, you know, 100 girls all auditioning as Audrey Hepburn or Julie Andrews,” Power says. “Eliza is so close to who I am in terms of how overly emotional she is, and how reactive she is, and how she sort of likes to storyboard her life.”

Friedman says the search for an Eliza was a long one. “Verity walked in, and she is authentic,” the director says. “Somebody hasn’t ironed her out.”

The accent question runs through the entire conversation. Cumming describes enduring “a low-level racism, actually,” in London over his accent and the assumptions people made about his education and background. In New York, the same qualities were celebrated. “[As] a baby actor, I was sort of told to squash it or hide it,” he says. “Once again, it’s kind of what the show is about, your authenticity and all that sort of stuff being your biggest gift.”

Power, who says she mostly works in retail when she isn’t acting, describes sizing up customers to decide whether she can talk in her own voice or needs her “shop voice.” When she does put it on, people ask if she is from Edinburgh. “I go, ‘No, no, I’m from Glasgow,’ and they’ll be, ‘God, your accent’s not strong at all,’ and I’m like, ‘That’s because I’m trying to make your life easier!'” she says. “Every Scottish person I know has had those.”

Friedman’s other big move is scale. She is turning a show that would normally need around 30 performers into a chamber piece with 18, swings included, and a smaller orchestra. “These things are too expensive to put on. So it’s really, really hard for these shows to go on and have a life,” she says. “I want to make it into a piece that speaks to us today in a beautifully concentrated, chiseled-down version.”

That fits Cumming’s ambitions for Pitlochry’s musicals, which he wants to stand on their own and travel beyond Perthshire. He has, he says, “no interest in doing musicals that are just sort of regurgitations of West End things.” For that to happen, the shows “need to have idiosyncrasy and passion and panache.”

Power says Friedman has treated the show from day one “as a play where we just so happen to also sing sometimes.” Cumming describes a director who keeps pushing and questioning, and who is specific early. “Her energy is very, what’s the word, effervescent,” he says. “So it’s an exciting room to be in.”

Cumming’s own profile has shifted since he took on hosting duties for “The Traitors,” a job he accepted on a whim, knowing nothing about competition reality shows. He built a character for it, and the result has brought him commercials and wider visibility. The lesson, he says, is much the same as the play’s: value your authenticity and go with your gut. “I went with something that everybody thought I was bonkers to do, and it’s turned out into this kind of amazing thing for me,” he says.

Along with Cumming and Power, the cast includes Martin Ball as Colonel Pickering, Alexander Newland as Alfred P. Doolittle, Juliet Cadzow as Mrs. Pearce, Julie Legrand as Mrs. Higgins and Alex James-Hatton as Freddy Eynsford-Hill. Tim Hatley designs, with choreography by Ashley Nottingham, lighting by Neil Austin and musical supervision by Joel Fram.

Asked what will land hardest with audiences now, Power points to the pressure on women to be polite, sound right and look a certain way, citing the lip filler and Botox ads that appear unsolicited on her phone. “It happened then, and it happens now, and it will sadly probably happen forever,” she says. “But it is something that we can take hold of and scrunch up in a ball and chuck away and go, ‘No, we’re fab as we are.'”

Democrats win Alaska Senate race two weeks after midterms counting began

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Mary Peltola defeated the state's Republican incumbent after a controversial race

Democrat Mary Peltola has won Alaska's hard-fought Senate race, completing a sweeping victory for the party in the US upper house in the midterm elections.

The win gives the Democrats a 56th Senate seat following a tumultuous and controversial election in Alaska, which featured alleged spoiler recruitment efforts and unprecedented court battles.

It took two full weeks of counting to call a winner in the 3 November poll due to the logistical challenge of collecting ballots from across the state's remote expanse.

It is the ninth Senate seat the Democrats have taken from Republicans in the midterms, giving the party control of the US Congress for the last two years of Donald Trump's presidency.

The Democrats also won back the House of Representatives, the lower chamber.

Peltola defeated the incumbent Republican senator, Dan S Sullivan, who is yet to comment.

In her victory statement, Peltola said the "status quo in DC is not working for Alaska".

She recalled speaking to "elders in tears about empty fish racks" and to parents in rural Alaska choosing between heating their homes and putting food on the table.

Peltola is the first Alaska Native elected to the US Senate, and is the first Democrat to win a US Senate seat in Alaska since 2008.

Donald Trump won the state by 13 points in the 2024 presidential election, but defeat for the Republicans in this year's US Senate race in the state caps off a string of losses in the midterms for the president's party.

Many voters across the United States cited cost-of-living issues as a factor that affected how they cast their ballots.

Trump says Alaska voters chose the wrong Dan Sullivan. What does the data show?

Could Alaska's fishing industry tilt Washington's balance of power?

Peltola's campaign had come under fire after the New York Times, external reported that her team worked with people who tried to recruit so-called spoiler candidates in an alleged attempt to confuse voters.

The alleged scheme led to a candidate named Daniel J Sullivan - a complete political unknown residing in a small coastal town who shared a name with the state's current US Senator and incumbent, Dan S Sullivan - contesting the race.

After legal challenges, state courts ruled that Daniel J Sullivan could remain on the ballot, and election officials distinguished them on election materials.

Peltola denied having knowledge of the alleged plot but her campaign swiftly came under fire, and both the US justice department and Alaska's Republican governor said they would launch investigations.

The Trump administration also filed a lawsuit attempting to stop the certification of the election results if the incumbent Republican Senator Sullivan lost.

A federal judge, though, dismissed the case, following legal challenges from Alaska election officials as well as the Peltola campaign and voting groups.

Due to Alaska's ranked-choice voting system, voters confused about which Dan Sullivan was the incumbent could have simply chosen both men, listing them as their first and second choices.

If they did so, after the first round of voting was complete, tabulation would have re-allocated any votes from Daniel J Sullivan - who stood little to no chance of winning - to Dan S Sullivan.

Experts told the BBC that this process made it difficult for any potential spoiler candidate to have any impact on the results.

An analysis by BBC Verify found that even if every vote for Daniel J Sullivan had gone to the incumbent, Dan S Sullivan, Peltola would still have won.

The Democratic win in Alaska cements the party's majority in the US Senate, as the party now has 56 seats to the Republicans' 44.

The most senior Democrat in the Senate, Chuck Schumer, said Peltola had run "a pitch-perfect campaign devoted to the issues Alaskans care most about". Hailing the Democrats' final tally in the Senate, he said: "56 seats was an unimaginable result even just a few months ago."

The Alaska seat was initially one of several toss-up races that appeared as a must-win for Democrats if they wanted to gain control of the US Senate. But by the time Peltola was called the winner, they had seats to spare.

With 99% of ballots counted, Peltola had slightly more than 50% of the votes, while Sullivan trailed on less than 46%.

Sullivan, a former Alaska attorney general and an ally of Trump, campaigned on his record of delivering energy and military investment for the state.

Peltola promised Alaskans a more independent voice in Washington.

"As a lifelong Alaskan who has dedicated my life to serving our state, there is no greater honor than having Alaskans' support in our fight to take on corporate greed, lower costs, and put Alaska first," she said in her victory statement.

"I will stand up to anyone to take on the rigged system in DC and deliver results for Alaska's working families."

Tom Hardy & Guy Ritchie’s Mob Thriller Officially Peaks 3 Days Before Finale

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Tom Hardy and Guy Ritchie's mob thriller hits its all-time rating with season 2's penultimate episode, just three days before its November 20 finale. The second season of this riveting crime drama has shown great improvement since its already successful debut season and is building a legitimate case for being one of the best series in its genre of the 2020s thus far. Hardy and Ritchie's star-studded show is the most-watched worldwide on Paramount+'s viewership charts right now, positioning it nicely for a phenomenal season 2 finale this week.

MobLand just matched its series-high peak IMDb score with an 8.7 rating on the ninth episode of its second season, which was recently released last Friday, November 13. This matches season 2, episode 7 as the highest-rated MobLand episode of all time on Paramount+, setting up its most anticipated finale episode yet this Friday. Hardy's protagonist, Harry Da Souza, has become the key player in a lethal chess match between the London-based Harrigon crime family and a new season 2 villain, Frankie Cooper, who has single-handedly brought the series to new heights with a menacing presence.

MobLand season 2 is officially starting to prove that its hot start was far from a fluke, delivering a masterful penultimate episode that has elevated the series overall. This week is the perfect opportunity to binge-watch the nine MobLand season 2 episodes available to stream right now on Paramount+, with audiences confirming that the series has only gotten better with each new installment. MobLand season 2's Rotten Tomatoes audience score has risen to 75% during its season 2 rollout, which began back on September 18 and is now finally arriving at its explosive conclusion more than two months later.

MobLand season 2, episode 9, "Nervous Breakdown," is as tense as it sounds, forcing Harry into a predicament in which his loyalties to the Harrigons and his actual family, mainly his wife and daughter, are tested to their most extreme levels. Harry's often reserved and polite demeanor is pushed past its breaking point in the latest MobLand episode, as Harry walks into a lion's den willing to sacrifice himself for the people he loves most. Although Harry's marriage has been on the rocks and his relationship with his daughter has been strained throughout MobLand season 2, Harry is inadvertently given a fantastic opportunity to right his past wrongs and determine exactly where his true priorities lie.

The only major concern about a new series-high MobLand episode is the high expectations that will now be placed on it heading into Friday's finale. With a third season already confirmed, MobLand has made it clear that it has plenty more left in the tank, combining some of the greatest minds in film and television into what is rapidly becoming an epic mob masterpiece. While Ritchie directs several episodes of MobLand and serves as an executive producer along with Hardy and co-creator Jez Butterworth, Lawrence Gough (The Gold, Band of Spies) directed season 2, episode 9, and will direct the tenth and final episode released this year.

Prime Video's 16-part action thriller hit has become a major franchise, with a second spinoff officially in development following its first prequel.

MobLand has only become more sophisticated in its second season, increasing its scale from a tit-for-tat turf war in season 1 to a wide-scale operation with multiple names in the hat. Without getting into spoiler territory, just about every MobLand actor who returned from season 1 has grown more into their roles, while season 2 newcomers bring formidable energy through an elite blend of performance and writing. MobLand is simply firing on all cylinders right now and is arguably the best series that both Ritchie and Hardy have ever been a part of, and its new series-high all-time IMDb episode score only speaks further to its excellence.

How New Hunger Games Prequel Changes The Original Movies

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Haymitch Abernathy knows how little winning the Hunger Games is worth. Katniss spends two movies discovering that her victory cannot guarantee Peeta’s safety, protect her family, or give her control over her future. Her mentor has lived with those limitations for decades. Woody Harrelson gives Haymitch’s warnings an exhausted impatience, as though he wishes she would stop making him explain something so painfully obvious. The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping will show the teenager who had to learn it himself, before he was expected to help anyone else survive.

Set 24 years before Katniss volunteers for Prim, the prequel follows 16-year-old Haymitch into the 50th Hunger Games. The Second Quarter Quell doubles the number of tributes, forcing him to compete against 47 other children. His name takes the uncertainty out of who survives, but the film’s confirmed resistance storyline gives viewers another reason to follow him. Haymitch attempts to bring down the Games from inside the arena. The man who later teaches Katniss how to please sponsors once tried to destroy the competition those sponsors were paying to watch.

By the time Katniss challenges Snow, Haymitch has spent decades watching the Games continue despite his attempt to stop them. Helping her means risking another failure, this time with children he has fought to keep alive. His warnings become easier to understand when viewers know what trying once already cost him. And when Catching Fire makes the victors eligible for the reaping again, the Capitol threatens to put him through another Quarter Quell after everything he endured to survive the first.

Winning the Hunger Games gives Haymitch a job that requires him to keep coming back. Each year, District 12 sends more children to the Capitol, and he is expected to help them survive. When Katniss and Peeta arrive, he has been doing this for more than two decades without bringing another victor home. His drinking is the first thing they notice. The years of unsuccessful attempts to save their predecessors are harder to see.

Sunrise on the Reaping will put Haymitch on the other side of that relationship. Before he had to explain sponsors and arena strategy to frightened teenagers, he was a tribute depending on other people’s help. Watching that experience could make his work in the original movies feel worse. He knows what the children need from him because he once needed it himself.

For Haymitch, returning to the Capitol means watching another pair of District 12 children go through the interviews, training, and goodbyes he once endured. He can prepare them for what happens next, but until Katniss and Peeta, none of them comes home.

Haymitch’s history with Katniss could begin with her father. In Suzanne Collins’ Sunrise on the Reaping, he and Burdock are friends long before Katniss is born. If the adaptation keeps that connection, Haymitch will be trying to save his old friend’s daughter when she arrives in the Capitol. Katniss has no reason to know that when she first sees him drinking on the train.

Her father’s death has already forced her to hunt for food and help raise Prim. Those responsibilities explain much of her impatience with Haymitch. She needs useful advice from the one person assigned to help her, and he barely seems willing to speak to her. Knowing that he once cared about her father would make his behavior more upsetting, even as it gives his eventual attachment to her a history the original films never explored.

It would also give Haymitch something to offer Katniss beyond advice about the Games. He remembers her father as a young person, before the mine explosion and the years her family spent struggling afterward. Their relationship could carry that possibility even when neither knows how to talk about it. Saving Katniss would mean bringing home the child of someone Haymitch can no longer see again.

Haymitch has to watch Snow threaten Katniss years after facing him himself. That alone makes their scenes in Catching Fire worth revisiting. When she tells Haymitch about Snow’s visit, she needs help figuring out how to keep her family safe. He has already spent much of his life under the same president, and reassuring her would mean making promises he cannot keep.

Snow has Haymitch’s mother, brother, and girlfriend killed after his victory. If the film retains those deaths, Katniss’s account of Snow threatening her loved ones would mean Haymitch is hearing a teenager describe something that already happened to him.

It would also change the experience of watching Snow enter her home. Katniss is still learning how far he will go. Viewers would know he has done this before, to the man she turns to for help afterward. Donald Sutherland’s delivery would be harder to stomach with that knowledge. Snow barely needs to explain the threat because Haymitch’s life already shows what it means.

In Catching Fire, Beetee’s plan ends with Katniss firing an electrified arrow into the arena’s force field. Sunrise on the Reaping could turn that escape into the payoff to an attempt made 25 years earlier. In Collins’s novel, Beetee helps Haymitch attempt to sabotage his arena. His own son, Ampert, is among the tributes that year. If the adaptation retains that storyline, Beetee’s involvement in Katniss’s escape will come after an earlier effort that failed to save his child.

That makes the lightning-tree plan much more personal. Beetee is returning to the arena’s machinery as a way to fight the Capitol, with Haymitch again involved in the conspiracy. Katniss does not know that history when she wraps the wire around her arrow. She is acting on an opportunity that people before her tried and failed to create.

The force field breaking in The Hunger Games: Sunrise of the Reaping would offer something the earlier Games could not. Haymitch survives his arena, but the competition continues. In Catching Fire, he finally helps get tributes out without letting the Hunger Games finish. Not only that, Beetee lives to see an arena destroyed instead of another child crowned.

Taylor Sheridan and Guy Ritchie Officially Battle for No. 1 Spot on Streaming

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Taylor Sheridan has spent the past several years building an impressive collection of television hits, but even with Yellowstone, Landman, and Lioness under his belt, there's something particularly satisfying about watching Sylvester Stallone become one of streaming's biggest crime bosses. The Oscar-nominated star has made Dwight Manfredi one of Paramount+'s most recognizable characters, with the former New York mobster steadily expanding his criminal empire across Oklahoma. But there's been some serious competition lately, particularly from Guy Ritchie's MobLand, which has been enjoying another successful season. Fortunately for Sheridan, his resident gangster has managed to reclaim the throne.

Tulsa King has officially overtaken MobLand to reach No. 1 on Paramount+'s global television chart, following the arrival of its fourth season. According to FlixPatrol rankings reported on October 19, the Stallone-led crime drama had occupied the top spot for two consecutive days, pushing MobLand into second place. More recently, Tulsa King also reached No. 1 on Paramount+'s US chart, with MobLand sitting at No. 3 in the November 14 rankings.

The series stars Sylvester Stallone (Rocky) as Dwight Manfredi, Martin Starr (Silicon Valley) as Bodhi, Jay Will (The Marvelous Mrs. Maisel) as Tyson Mitchell, Max Casella (The Sopranos) as Armand Truisi, and Dana Delany (Desperate Housewives) as Margaret Devereaux. Meanwhile, MobLand stars Tom Hardy (Venom) as Harry Da Souza, Pierce Brosnan (GoldenEye) as Conrad Harrigan, and Helen Mirren (The Queen) as Maeve Harrigan.

The show that claimed the most of your answers is the world you were built for. If two tied, both are shown — you're complicated enough to straddle two Sheridan universes.

You are a Dutton — or you might as well be. You understand that some things are worth protecting at any cost, and that the modern world's indifference to history, to land, to legacy, is not something you're willing to accept quietly. You lead from the front, you carry your family's weight without complaint, and when someone threatens what's yours, you don't escalate — you finish it. You're not cruel. But you are absolute. In Yellowstone's world, that combination of ferocity and loyalty doesn't make you a villain. It makes you the only thing standing between everything that matters and everyone who wants to take it.

You thrive in the chaos of high-stakes negotiation, where the money is enormous, the margins are thin, and the wrong word in the wrong room can cost everyone everything. You're a fixer — the person called when a situation is already on fire and needs someone with the nerve to walk into it. West Texas oil country rewards exactly what you are: sharp, adaptable, unsentimental, and absolutely clear-eyed about what people want and what they'll do to get it. You're not naive enough to think this world is fair. You're smart enough to be the one deciding who it's fair to.

You are a Dwight Manfredi — someone who has served their time, paid their dues, and arrived somewhere unexpected with nothing but their reputation and their wits. You adapt without losing yourself. You build loyalty through respect rather than fear, though you're not above reminding people that the two aren't mutually exclusive. Tulsa King is for people who are still standing when everyone assumed they'd be finished — who find, in an unfamiliar place, that they're more capable than the world gave them credit for. You don't need a throne. You build one, wherever you happen to land.

You carry the weight of a system that is broken by design, and you do it anyway — because someone has to, and because you're the only one positioned to do it without the whole thing collapsing. Mike McLusky's world is for people who are comfortable operating where there are no good options, only less catastrophic ones. You speak every language: law enforcement, criminal, political, human. That fluency makes you invaluable and it makes you a target. You've made your peace with both. Mayor of Kingstown belongs to people who understand that keeping the peace is not the same as being at peace — and who do the job regardless.

That's not all, though. Frisco King, the highly anticipated Tulsa King spin-off starring Samuel L. Jackson, will officially premiere on Paramount+ on December 18, 2026, in a two-episode premiere, while Paramount also kindly dropped the first teaser for the series as well as the announcement. Written by Sheridan, Frisco King follows seasoned New Orleans gangster Lee Washington as he lands in Frisco, Texas, and discovers a hustler’s playground hidden beneath the polished surface of American suburbia.

Stallone will be reprising his role as Dwight 'The General' Manfredi in a number of episodes. In fact, thanks to Collider's Steve Weintraub, we know exactly how many episodes it'll be: "I am in two and a half episodes." Stallone also discussed his experience working alongside Jackson, comparing their partnership to two heavyweight boxers who know exactly what they're doing. He told Collider:

"You know, Sam, it's kind of like two racers in identical cars going at the same speed. You don't feel you're superior, or you're working with a neophyte, or someone who's green. You got a guy here that's had 50 years' experience, so it's kind of like Muhammad Ali and Joe Fraser. You better be on your game, or you're gonna get punched out."

All four seasons of Tulsa King are streaming on Paramount+, alongside both seasons of MobLand. Stay tuned to Collider for all the latest news and updates on the biggest and best movies and shows heading to your favorite streaming platforms soon.

Tulsa King follows New York mafia capo Dwight “The General” Manfredi (Stallone), just after he is released from prison after 25 years and unceremoniously exiled by his boss to set up shop in Tulsa, Oklahoma.

10 Classic Thriller Books Worth Reading Again and Again

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

If you read books once, even great ones, that’s good. That’s better than not reading anything at all. It’s hard to find the time sometimes to read things fully, but it is rewarding once you get into the habit, because even if books get adaptations into other mediums, those adaptations aren’t always ideal (and often, even when they are done well, there’s still a lot you can only get from the source material).

So, if you’ve got even more time on your hands, or otherwise feel capable of re-reading a few books, these ones are probably worth tackling. Or, well, re-tackling. Reading again? Whatever sounds better. These are great thriller books that are rewarding to read again and again, often because they're overwhelming and heavy on details and/or they have some pretty great plot twists that might be even easier to admire once you’ve read the book one time and the shock of those revelations has worn off.

The Hunt for Red October came out when the Cold War was still ongoing, and so while it might not be as timely now, it’s an interesting snapshot of the times. But more importantly, it holds up as a thriller regardless of the broader conflict it’s set against, being the first of the Jack Ryan books, and one with a great premise, too: there’s a Soviet submarine captain who goes rogue while being equipped with some immense weaponry, and chaos ensues.

Well, people work to stop things from getting too chaotic, but The Hunt for Red October is certainly tense, and it’s written in a way where you can believe something bad will happen at any point (even if you know, reading post-1984, that Jack Ryan has a fair few more books/events to get wrapped up in). It’s a strong thriller/war novel, and a good starting point for Tom Clancy, since it was his first published novel and all.

If you like science fiction, you can’t go wrong with the better-known Michael Crichton novels, since he did very much like writing within this genre, and Jurassic Park is no exception… though this one might be a bit more thrilling than some of his earlier hard sci-fi books. Make no mistake: there’s still a lot of science in this science fiction, but the whole bringing dinosaurs back to life thing does ultimately give Jurassic Park a bit more in the excitement department.

As long as you're not expecting something as thrilling or broadly enjoyable as the film adaptation (which is tense for sure, but gentler overall), then the original Jurassic Park is well worth diving into. There’s a lot here, and so as with the case with all books overflowing with ideas, going back to them and reading it again is always helpful.

There was a famed movie adaptation of Rebecca just a couple of years after the book came out, and it’s notable for being the only Alfred Hitchcock-directed film to win Best Picture. On its own, though, and just looking at the book, it’s a classic with or without that noteworthy movie, since it’s one of the best gothic novels of all time, and one with a good deal of mystery, crime, and romance elements, alongside some other things.

You can call it a thriller, but perhaps more of a slow-burn one that rewards patience, and then it has enough by way of reveals that re-reading Rebecca will also prove rewarding. It’s about a young woman whose first name is never learned, and she becomes the second wife of a mysterious man who shows increasingly troubling signs that he’s haunted by the death of his first wife. Some patience is needed to go with it while it’s all unfolding, but it’s more than worth it and everything.

Living up to its title, The Running Man keeps things going at a very fast pace, and that’s extra appropriate when you learn that Stephen King (under his Richard Bachman pseudonym) wrote this in a shorter period of time than it might take you to read some of the author’s longest books. The similarly titled The Long Walk might be more horrifying, and ultimately more psychologically devastating (as well as better overall), but The Running Man has more thrills.

Stephen King (under his Richard Bachman pseudonym) wrote this in a shorter period of time than it might take you to read some of the author’s longest books.

It is unapologetically a thriller alongside being a work of dystopian science fiction, with the premise here involving one man’s struggles to survive a deadly competition that’s like extreme reality TV, and involves people trying to kill you the whole time. It’s a high-risk/high-reward show (well, on paper, there are rewards), and it’s ultimately such a wonderfully quick read, being the sort of thing that’s hard to put down once you start. So, the idea of returning to it doesn’t feel like too much of a leap, nor an especially big time commitment.

The Black Dahlia is the first book in the L.A. Quartet series, though the books generally have different characters at their centers, with references and thematic similarities, sure, but not much by way of continuity between books until you get to some shared narrative elements between books #3 and #4 (L.A. Confidential and White Jazz). All four books get progressively more complicated, too, meaning The Black Dahlia feels refreshingly straightforward in comparison, even if it’s quite dense and harrowing by most other standards.

It’s the most focused on a real-life crime out of any book in the quartet, with the titular case being infamously grisly and unsolved: that of the murder of Elizabeth Short, who became known as the Black Dahlia. There are fictional characters who get wrapped up in this real-life murder case, and it all makes for a great psychological thriller book dealing with obsession, and comparable to Rebecca in the sense that it feels like it’s a book about being haunted, too (though here, not in a horror/supernatural sense).

If you judge it as a postmodern book, House of Leaves is not one of the hardest to follow, so long as you get used to some of the strange stylistic and formatting decisions. By most other metrics, though, it’s pretty full-on and confounding, since it’s about a guy researching another guy who was researching a documentary film about a strange sort of haunted house.

But then different discoveries are made at different levels, and lots of things cross over and/or appear uncertain. The book itself does strange things that make you feel like you're going a bit mad as well, like all the characters, and so calling House of Leaves exceptionally immersive would be quite an understatement. Still, it’s also extremely entertaining and ambitious with all the genres and emotional beats it goes for, so diving into it all again at some point feels pretty much essential and undoubtedly rewarding.

The most legendary of the Hannibal Lecter books would have to be The Silence of the Lambs, and sure, that’s also something you can say about all the movies featuring the character. The Silence of the Lambs just has the most by way of stuff going on, with Lecter himself being imprisoned and used by an FBI trainee who is after another serial killer actually at large… or is she being used by him?

Or both? You know the drill. You’ve either read the book or seen the movie, or perhaps you’ve done both, because that’s always very much worth doing. There’s a great dynamic here between Lecter and Clarice Starling, as the two main characters of the book, and then the more outward/traditional villain, Buffalo Bill, is no slouch in the “gets-your-heart-racing-and-your-stomach-feeling-queasy” department. Sure, that department does need a catchier name, but you get the sentiment, right?

The Talented Mr. Ripley centers on a man named Tom Ripley, and he is talented in the sense that he definitely knows how to be a con artist, and he continues his conning ways in a whole series of books. But The Talented Mr. Ripley is the first, and probably the best, being about what happens when Tom becomes infatuated with another man named Dickie Greenleaf, aspiring to have just about everything that Dickie has.

If you're worried about spoilers for a 70-year-old book… uh, well, there’s something dramatic that happens, maybe more dramatic than in most books, and then there’s a whole series of consequences that follow that event. It’s textbook stuff maybe, but also executed so well, with Patricia Highsmith being one of the all-time greats when it came to crafting this kind of psychological thriller/crime story.

Stephen King knows his way around the horror genre, without a doubt, and Misery does see him tackling horror while also doing what’s probably his strongest effort as far as ventures into psychological thriller territory go. Here, things also feel pretty grounded, since Misery has a villain who’s famously terrifying without having any fantastical or otherworldly powers, and the protagonist is memorably at odds with her the whole time, since he’s an author she’s a fan of, and she’s kidnapped him.

From there, things get more intense and gruesome, and protagonist Paul Sheldon is indeed one of the Stephen King characters who’s gone through the most misery (ha), and that’s saying something. Overall, Misery is one of the very best Stephen King books, and so long as you’ve got the stomach for some of the grislier scenes, it’s probably one worth returning to sooner rather than later.

Agatha Christie is the quintessential murder-mystery writer, and so her books are very thrilling, and sure, some – like And Then There Were None – cross over a little genre-wise (there are some borderline-horror elements here, honestly). In any event, that level of praise is not really something that can be said casually, but she earns it, and this novel is perhaps her best. It’s got the greatest premise imaginable for such a book, since it’s about a bunch of people on an island getting murdered one by one.

The slowest it gets is right at the start, because you do need to have some introductions for the characters (or murder victims), and you care just enough before the murdering and dying starts happening. And Then There Were None is truly relentless once it gets going, and the re-readability of it all is obvious: like, it’s fun to be surprised the first time around, but then the second time, it’s neat to appreciate all the foreshadowing and better understand how Christie toys with you the first time around.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Caleb Williams remains frustrated with four interception game

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

In his last game, Caleb Williams threw four interceptions. The quarterback has not had a chance to truly put it behind him yet as the Bears had an off week in Week 10.

He called two of the picks “funky” and a third “a bad decision.”

“The biggest thing is, the frustration comes from the feeling of, for me, personally, I always don’t want to let my teammates down,” Williams said, via Courtney Cronin of ESPN. “That’s always big for me. And in that situation, in those situations, I feel like I did. And so, that’s the frustrating part that lingers.”

Williams had thrown four total interceptions in the 11 games before the Bucs game, dating to Nov. 28, 2025. He had never thrown more than two in a game.

“He’s playing fast right now,” Johnson said. “He’s got a great approach. He’s doing a good job downloading the game plan. We don’t feel like we’re hindered in any way as a coaching staff from the schematics of what we’re able to put in, the details, the alerts. He’s just able to digest a lot more. He’s seeing coverage really well with where his eyes should be tied into his feet also. So, we’re really encouraged with where he’s at. We’ve just got to continue to push that envelope and see how far it will take us.”

During his break, Williams’ close friend, Cubs outfielder Pete Crow-Armstrong, won the National League MVP award. Williams’ odds to win NFL MVP are +3500.

Williams was asked whether Chicago is big enough to handle two MVPs.

After Gilligan's Island, Jim Backus Appeared In This 1977 Disney Cult Classic

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

"Gilligan's Island" came to an end after three seasons in 1967, and while he reprised the role of Thurston Howell III in two animated "Gilligan's Island" spin-offs and three TV continuation movies, Jim Backus found time for plenty of other projects. That included two collaborations with director Don Chaffey.

The first of these saw Chaffey, of "Jason and the Argonauts" fame, direct Backus in the 1977 live action/animation hybrid "Pete's Dragon." /Film previously dubbed "Pete's Dragon" a fascinating and surprisingly mean Disney oddity, and that's largely true — though watching this movie as a kid I don't recall being particularly struck by the meanness of it. Looking back, however, there were certainly some surprisingly dark moments in the film, including the opening musical number in which a gang of hillbillies sing enthusiastically about recapturing their escaped child slave and "tying him screaming to a railroad track."

Disney was going through a transitional phase at the time, during which things just seemed to get a little more grim than usual. "Pete's Dragon" debuted the same year as another Disney movie with some dreary overtones: "The Rescuers." But the former was particularly odd for combining its songs about child slavery with a wholesome throwback musical vibe and a lovable cartoon dragon overlaid on live-action scenes. It resulted in a film that seemed simultaneously ahead of its time technically and yet felt like one big anachronism in a year that also gave us two of the greatest sci-fi movies of all time. Having Thurston Howell pop up just added to the disorientation, even if Backus was only in a few scenes.

"Pete's Dragon" stars Sean Marshall as young orphan Pete, who escapes from the aforementioned hillbillies, the Gogans, with the help of his invisible dragon friend, Elliott. Pete manages to avoid being tied screaming to the railroad tracks and makes it to the Maine fishing village of Passamaquoddy. There, he meets lighthouse keeper Lampie (Mickey Rooney), and his kind adult daughter Nora, (Helen Reddy), who take in Pete and become the loving family he's always needed.

Meanwhile, traveling snake oil salesman Doc Terminus (Jim Dale) and his assistant Hoagy (Red Buttons) arrive in Passamaquoddy and win over the townspeople with their grift. When Dale's villain discovers the existence of Elliott, however, he becomes obsessed with capturing the beast to concoct new tonics. Soon, the Gogans also arrive to claim Pete, before forming an alliance with Terminus to recapture both their former child slave and Elliott.

Jim Backus appears in a few brief scenes as the Mayor of Passamaquoddy, who twice finds himself the victim of an invisible Elliot's antics. Backus isn't in the film much, but does a decent job with the physical comedy of his scenes, recalling moments from his "Gilligan's Island" years. The actor previously lent his voice talents, honed over several decades of playing one of animation's most famous characters, Mr. Magoo, to the 1951 Disney animated short "Plutopia" and later played Timothy Forsythe in the 1972 live-action Disney comedy "Now You See Him, Now You Don't." The Mayor in "Pete's Dragon" was his final Disney role — though he was referenced in Disney's attempt to revive Mr. Magoo with a live action update that Roger Ebert called "transcendently bad." Happily, the same can't be said for "Pete's Dragon," even if it has gone mostly overlooked.

Originally conceived as an episode of "Walt Disney's Disneyland" back in the mid-'50s, "Pete's Dragon" was scrapped before eventually being revisited in the mid-'70s. By this time, pop culture looked a lot different than two decades prior. 1977 was, of course, the year a little movie titled "Star Wars" debuted, as well as Steven Spielberg's "Close Encounters of the Third Kind."

Released on November 3, 1977, "Pete's Dragon," a throwback musical set in early 1900s Maine and shot on the Disney backlot, found itself sandwiched between two of the best sci-fi movies of all time. "Star Wars" and "Close Encounters" were both symbolic of mass audiences' changing tastes as sci-fi came to rule the world. As such, "Pete's Dragon" went mostly overlooked.

That is, the film has been mostly overlooked since its release. In 1977 it actually made Disney a decent profit, earning $35.4 million upon its 1977 debut and a further $4.1million when it was re-released in 1984. On a $10 million budget, that ain't bad. The movie also earned two Oscar nominations, one for musical scoring and another for original song. Still, "Pete's Dragon" never became a Disney classic, which is a shame because I, and I'm sure others, were enchanted by it as a kid.

Meanwhile, Jim Backus went on to collaborate with Don Chaffey a second time in the 1979 sci-fi comedy "C.H.O.M.P.S. " It, too went mostly overlooked. Some 37 years later in 2016, we got a "Pete's Dragon" remake which was similarly underrated and is a must-watch for "How to Train your Dragon" fans. But the original is such a charming little oddity that it could never truly be outdone. If you fancy checking it out, the film is available to watch on Disney+.

IDA Awards Nominations: ‘Concrete Land’ Lands 6, With ‘To Hold A Mountain’ Close Behind; Elizabeth Holmes Doc In The Mix

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

Concrete Land, which has received little attention in the U.S. so far, surprised with a leading six nominations Tuesday as the 42nd IDA Documentary Awards announced its nominees. Sundance winner To Hold a Mountain came in close behind with five.

Concrete Land, directed by Asmahan Bkerat, centers on the the Najar family, “one of the last Afro-Palestinian Bedouin families near Amman,” who face eviction as luxury villas encroach on their land. It won the Grierson Award at the BFI London Film Festival last month but has otherwise largely gone under the radar. Along with Best Documentary Feature, it is nominated for Best Production, Best Documentary Not in the English Language, Best Editing, Best Sound Design, and Best Writing. Related Stories Documentary Oscar Contender 'To Hold A Mountain' To Open In Theaters In December Ahead Of HBO Debut Documentary IDA Documentary Awards Shortlists: 'You Can See Everything,' 'To Hold A Mountain,' 'Nuisance Bear' Make Features Cut, But What Got Snubbed?

Joining Concrete Land in the marquee Best Documentary Feature category is To Hold a Mountain, directed by Biljana Tutorov and Petar Glomazić. That film – Montenegro’s choice for the Oscar category of Best International Feature – tells the story of Gara, a remarkable woman who is determined to shield her daughter from harm while simultaneously attempting to prevent the Montenegrin government from turning beloved mountain pastures into a military training ground. Watch on Deadline

Also nominated for Best Documentary Feature: A Fox Under a Pink Moon, directed by Mehrdad Oskouei and Soraya Akhlaghi; Rehearsals for a Revolution, directed by Pegah Ahangarani, and You Can See Everything, directed by Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim.

The latter film, the box office smash about imprisoned Theranos founder Elizabeth Holmes, earned three nominations overall, including Best Director and Best Original Music Score, recognizing composer Ari Balouzian. Rehearsals for a Revolution and A Fox Under a Pink Moon landed four nominations apiece.

Rehearsals for a Revolution (primarily in Farsi), A Fox Under a Pink Moon (in Farsi, Dari, and Turkish) and Concrete Land (primarily in Arabic) earned nominations for Best Documentary Not in the English Language. Curiously, To Hold a Mountain, in Montenegrin, didn’t earn a nomination in that category. Had it done so, it would have tied with Concrete Land for a leading six nominations.

Birds of War, directed by Janay Boulos and Abd Alkader Habak, scored three nominations – for Best Documentary Not in the English Language, Best Writing (for Jonathan Key, Janay Boulos, and Abd Alkader Habak), and Best Original Music Score, recognizing composers Harpal Mudhar and Darren Sng.

My Undesirable Friends: Part II – Exile, Julia Loktev’s follow up to her Oscar-shortlisted My Undesirable Friends: Part I – Last Air in Moscow, earned two nominations: for Best Director and Best Editing (recognizing Loktev and Michael Taylor). Closure, directed by Michał Marczak, and Nuisance Bear, directed by Gabriela Osio Vanden and Jack Weisman, both earned two nominations each, for Best Cinematography and Best Sound Design.

Also of note are nominations for Oscar winner Laura Poitras – for her short documentary They’re Here, co-directed with Rachel Lauren Mueller; Oscar winner Ahmir “Questlove” Thompson, nominated for Best Music Documentary for Earth, Wind & Fire (To Be Celestial vs. That’s the Weight of the World), and Oscar winner Morgan Neville, nominated for Best Music Documentary for Man on the Run. Prominent Hollywood hitmakers Baz Luhrmann and Paris Barclay earned nominations for their music documentaries – Luhrmann for EPiC: Elvis Presley in Concert, and Barclay for Billy Preston: That’s The Way God Planned It.

The 42nd IDA Documentary Awards will take place Friday, December 4, at The Ebell of Los Angeles, hosted by Tabitha Jackson, head of Film Forum and former director of the Sundance Film Festival.

In a statement, IDA board president Ina Fichman said, “I couldn’t be more excited to celebrate these meaningful and creative documentaries. It’s a challenging time for documentary makers, but also a really inspiring moment for IDA and for the field. This year we see nominees whose deeply personal stories share narratives of resistance and exile, and we see artful, innovative filmmaking that is capturing new and robust audiences. I hope many will join us on December 4th as we honor our incredible community.”

The IDA says it received a record 650 entries in all categories from 95 countries, “an increase over last year both in the total number of entries and the countries represented. This year, 32 countries are represented among the nominees.” IDA members from around the world vote to determine the winners in the Best Feature Documentary and Best Short Documentary categories (balloting began November 17 and extends to November 29). All other category winners are selected by juries representing the international documentary community.

These are the nominees announced on Tuesday:

IDA Documentary Awards 2026 Nominations: ‘You Can See Everything,’ ‘Concrete Land,’ and More Earn Nods

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

On Tuesday, November 17, the International Documentary Association (IDA) announced the nominees for its 42nd annual IDA Documentary Awards, including everything from the highly publicized blockbuster “You Can See Everything” to under-the-radar Best Documentary Feature contenders like “Concrete Land.”

“I couldn’t be more excited to celebrate these meaningful and creative documentaries. It’s a challenging time for documentary makers, but also a really inspiring moment for IDA and for the field,” IDA Board President Ina Fichman said in a statement. “This year we see nominees whose deeply personal stories share narratives of resistance and exile, and we see artful, innovative filmmaking that is capturing new and robust audiences. I hope many will join us on December 4 as we honor our incredible community.” Related Stories A24’s TV Division Is Quietly Having as Strong a Year as Its Film Team ‘The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth’ Throws Netflix’s Oscar Campaign a Curveball

The 2026 IDA Documentary Awards will also present the following special honors: the Career Achievement Award for the late Chilean filmmaker Patricio Guzmán; the Truth to Power Award for Amy Goodman and Juan González of Democracy Now!; the Emerging Filmmaker Award for Maia Lekow and Christopher King; and the Amicus Award for Cannes Docs, the documentary program of the Marché du Film led by Pierre-Alexis Chevit.

This year, IDA received a record 650 entries in all categories from 95 countries, an increase over last year both in the total number of entries and the countries represented. 32 countries are represented among the final lineup of nominees.

IDA members from around the world will vote for the winners in the Best Feature Documentary and Best Short Documentary categories from November 17 to November 29, 2026. All other category winners are selected by juries representing the international documentary community.

The Awards will take place December 4, 2026, at The Ebell of Los Angeles, hosted for the second year in a row by documentary field leader Tabitha Jackson.

Nominees in each category are listed in alphabetical order by film title.

“Concrete Land”Jordan, PalestineDirector: Asmahan BkeratProducers: Ban Maraqa, Asmahan Bkerat

“A Fox Under a Pink Moon”Iran, Türkiye, Greece | Kino LorberDirectors: Mehrdad Oskouei, Soraya AkhlaghiProducers: Mehrdad Oskouei, Siavash Jamali

7 Greatest Action Movies That Are Also Biopics, Ranked

📅 Nov 18, 2026  |  Category: Entertainment  |  Read Source →

It is not often thought of to associate action films with biographical dramas, but the two can intersect more often than not. There is a lineage of classic epics that are at least in part inspired by real stories, and many of them have been embellished over time. While many of these action epics are more based on religion than they are on history, which is the case with films like Ben-Hur or The Robe, they certainly kicked off an interest in using stories passed down by generations as a creative influence.

It can sometimes be hard to define a film if it does not entirely qualify for both categories. Lawrence of Arabia is a classic biopic that contains a lot of action, but might not have enough for it to be fully defined as an “action film.” Similarly, an action classic like Gladiator is loosely based on a true story, but contains too many factual inaccuracies for it to realistically be considered a biopic. Although the two definitions can be fungible, they crossover more times than some might expect.

Escape From Alcatraz was one of the greatest collaborations between Clint Eastwood and director Don Siegel, who had previously made the first Dirty Harry film together in addition to several other Westerns. The film is based on the true story of a 1962 prison escape in which three inmates on Alcatraz Island devised a scheme to break out of the notoriously secure facility. Eastwood stars as the ringleader of the group, and the supporting cast included Danny Glover in his first film role.

Escape from Alcatraz offered insights into the most mysterious prison in the United States, and attempted to depict it with more accuracy than the more sensationalist productions of films like The Rock. Although Eastwood had already built up a screen persona of someone who plays tough, morally flexible characters, Escape from Alcatraz saw him effectively mirroring a figure from the recent past, giving one of his best performances from a film that he did not also direct.

Bloodsport was widely marketed as being based on a true story, even if the facts regarding Frank Lux have been heavily debated, and in all likelihood somewhat fictionalized. There are nonetheless a number of great biopics that play fast and loose with the facts, and the inaccuracies involving Lux’s records do not prevent Bloodsport from being one of the most compelling martial-arts spectacles of all-time.

Jean-Claude Van Damme is not exactly an actor with a tremendous amount of range, but his skills as an actual martial artist made him perfect to star in this gritty thriller about a secretive fighting ring that accepts the best candidates in the world. Van Damme would go on to star in several other martial arts films, but the simplicity and unabashed brutality of Bloodsport made it a cult classic within the subgenre of late ‘80s fighting movies.

Braveheart is one of the breakaway successes of the ‘90s as it became a word-of-mouth hit in the summer of 1995 that turned into a true sensation, winning the Academy Award for Best Picture alongside a trophy for Best Director for Mel Gibson. Although the film has been heavily debated by historians for many of its perceived inaccuracies about the actual revolution by William Wallace, Braveheart certainly captures the sentiment of the Scottish rebels who were determined to be freed of English rule. Although Gibson’s only directorial credit up until that point had been the earnest drama The Man Without A Face, he certainly was able to transition into making a sweeping historical epic.

Braveheart captures the spirit of war in all of its ugliness in a successful way, as it offers maximum bloodshed without lionizing the act of violence itself; the story of the revolution begins as a simple act of revenge on the part of Wallace, and it blows up into a full-on political siege. While Gibson is by no means a subtle filmmaker and there are a number of rather obvious allusions to Christinaity, they felt less glaring back in 1995 when he had not yet reiterated his prior work.

Rescue Dawn is a curious project from Werner Herzog, who had previously made a documentary in 1997 about the same subject, Dieter Dengler, a German-American pilot who was shot down in the middle of the Vietnam War and forced to survive imprisonment and isolation. Although Herzog’s documentary had effectively captured the events of Dengler’s life and pointed to the context of why his rescue was so challenging, the narrative film is a more visceral experience that explores the minutia of being lost in the wilderness. The most important decision was casting Christian Bale to play Dengler, as at this point he had already attracted renown for his ability to physically transform.

Bale perfectly captures the spirit of a patriotic man who is hollowed-out by dispirited bleakness, and completely gives himself over to the part. Although Bale ended up getting the most attention, Rescue Dawn features an extraordinary supporting cast that includes brilliant supporting performances from Steve Zahn and Jeremy Davies.

The Revenant was based on the somewhat obscure story of Hugh Glass, a tracker in the early 19th century who was abandoned by his fellow men during a mission and left to die after his own son is killed by the deceitful con artist Fitzgerald (Tom Hardy). Given that it was an Alejandro Gonzalez Inarritu film, there is a tremendous amount of artistry relating to pain and suffering by means of the natural world; Leonardo DiCaprio completely transformed in an unrecognizable performance to play Glass, who is nearly killed by a bear before he grows up the strength to make the long journey in order to kill Fitzgerald.

The Revenant is an untraditional film for everyone involved, as it saw DiCaprio destroying his star persona to play a humbled man who calls upon ethereal strength in order to seek balance. It’s a revenge thriller that questions the very nature of vengeance, and it uses Glass’ violent odyssey as a means of exploring all the evil that exists within the American West, including the genocide of indigenious people and the destruction of nature. Rarely has a film of 156 minutes felt so relentlessly compelling, which is in large part due to the Oscar-winning cinematography of Emmanuel Lubezki, who used natural lighting to make the depiction of the Great Plains even more authentic.

Hacksaw Ridge was a comeback vehicle for Gibson after a period of Hollywood exile that briefly made it seem like he was going to be taken seriously again, at least until he started making more garbage like Flight Risk. Although it is unfortunate that Gibson seems intent on burning more bridges instead of actually seeking forgiveness, Hacksaw Ridge is a strong example of what he can do when he’s trained on telling an inherently compelling historical story that has strong moral takeaways. The film explores the plight of Desmond Doss, a drafted man in World War II who is played by Andrew Garfield. While Doss’ religious principles prevented him from touching a weapon, he agreed to serve the army as a medic, and saved countless lives in the process.

Hacksaw Ridge is well-drawn as a three-act hero’s journey arc that dedicates its first third to showing Doss’ upbringing, its second section examining the conflicts he came into with the most belligerent members of the army, and a stunning finale that includes some of the most gripping World War II combat footage ever brought to the screen. Although the film is stunningly violent in a way that does not shy away from the sheer brutality of wartime activity within the Pacific Theater, Garfield gives a deeply sensitive and moving performance that embodies the messianic themes at play. The entire cast is quite strong, with Hugo Weaving in particular giving an unforgettable performance as Doss’ father.

Napoleon is an interesting historical epic that was many years in the making, given that there have been many attempts to bring to life one of the most famous military leaders of all-time. Ridley Scott obviously has a vested interest in telling grandiose historical stories, and Napoleon allowed him to focus solely on the career of one man in a way that he wasn’t able to with more ensemble-based epics like Kingdom of Heaven and The Last Duel. The surprise of Napoleon is that Scott was more interested in the perception of a historical icon than nailing all of the facts; Joaquin Phoenix does not look or sound anything like the real Napoleon (and doesn’t even attempt to do a French accent), but the film still works as a harrowing character study.

Napoleon explores the extent of ego, as it uses the framework of a “great man” biopic to question why such vain men seek greatness. As is often the case, Scott made a much longer director’s cut that is superior.
